On 31/10/2026 15:16, Eli Zaretskii wrote:

>> But DEFUN allows you to define about any Lisp function, just in C. Like,
>> is 'android-detect-keyboard' a primitive? Possibly not.
> 
> It is, according to the ELisp manual:
> 
>    “primitive”
>         A function which is callable from Lisp but is actually written in
>         C.  Primitives are also called “built-in functions”, or “subrs”.
>         Examples include functions like ‘car’ and ‘append’.  In addition,
>         all special forms (see below) are also considered primitives.

AFAIK outside of Emacs Lisp the term "primitive" is used for a more 
compact set of functions.

In any case, the user wouldn't necessarily be familiar with the Elisp 
manual, if they themselves don't write Elisp. Our lisp of kinds has that 
entry anyway.

>>> Right.  In the end, simplicity won me, and I don't think the current
>>> single list is such a bad idea, don't you agree?
>>
>> It feels a little long now, and with new kinds planned to be added, that
>> would likely get more so.
> 
> Which new kinds are planned?

xref-backend-xref-kinds (etags.el:2286) says:

     ;; FIXME: Add more kinds for other languages:
     ;; HTML: title, h1, h2, h3, name=, id=

>> So unless we manage to reclassify the existing ones into a more compact
>> set of kinds, I think we should try that dynamic building of the list.
>>
>> Two options that I see:
>> - Base on the list of files in TAGS, by file extensions. Then you won't
>> see kinds not applicable to file types in the project, but still no
>> guarantee that some kinds won't show 0 identifiers.
> 
> I remind you that it is not simple to determine the language from
> file-name extensions alone.  So this might produce false negatives,
> with the result that some kinds are removed from the list when they
> shouldn't be.

That issue would both affect the list of kinds (as proposed), but also 
case-folding for identifier completions with kinds (WIP).

Whatever kinds would be missed due to the above logic, would have empty 
list of completions already. But that seems possible to improve, for 
both uses. If at least we could rely on the file-name->major-mode 
mappings. Just using file extensions would be much faster, though.

>> - Scan through TAGS for every regexp. Likely to be slower; the expense
>> would vary by project.
> 
> Yes, and that's just to suggest the list for selecting a kind.  Not
> sure it's worthwhile.
> 
> Maybe we should wait until we have some actual user feedback?

Okay, sure.

Hi,

I find the org-present package from nongnu ELPA to be quite useful in 
discussions and to sort out my thoughts. I'm creating a fork [1] to add 
some features (like hiding local variables) and to update the code 
replacing calls to obsolete functions, etc.

When I load the package on my emacs-32 (with native compilation enabled) 
using use-package :vc, I get a lot of "not known" native compilation 
warnings, despite using declare-function (which silenced the 
byte-compile warnings).

I have added no-native-compile: t as a local variable, but continue to 
get native compile errors.

I'm a bit lost (as you might derive from the message ;-) ) and would 
appreciate a moment of guidance, because after reading the documentation 
I feel even more confused...

Thanks, /PA

[1] https://codeberg.org/paaguti/org-present.

> Date: Sun, 1 Nov 2026 09:24:50 +0100
> From: "Pedro A. Aranda" <paaguti@gmail.com>
> 
> I find the org-present package from nongnu ELPA to be quite useful in 
> discussions and to sort out my thoughts. I'm creating a fork [1] to add 
> some features (like hiding local variables) and to update the code 
> replacing calls to obsolete functions, etc.
> 
> When I load the package on my emacs-32 (with native compilation enabled) 
> using use-package :vc, I get a lot of "not known" native compilation 
> warnings, despite using declare-function (which silenced the 
> byte-compile warnings).
> 
> I have added no-native-compile: t as a local variable, but continue to 
> get native compile errors.
> 
> I'm a bit lost (as you might derive from the message ;-) ) and would 
> appreciate a moment of guidance, because after reading the documentation 
> I feel even more confused...

Each warning most probably means that some 'require' is missing.
Native compilation compiles each Lisp file in a separate fresh
instance of Emacs, so any dependency packages that are assumed to be
already loaded, aren't.  If this is the reason, the solution is to add
the missing 'require's.

I don't see how can we help you more than this general advice, since
you didn't show a single warning of those you get.

Hi ELi,
thanks for taking the time to read and respond!

I have found the root cause:

I wasn't adding :rev :latest to use-package and it didn't take the 
correct commit.

Maybe highlighting "latest *release* commit" vs "latest commit" in

https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/use-package.html#Install-package

could help "fast readers" ;-)

Thanks again & a new lesson learnt today,
/PA

Phil Hagelberg [29/Oct  8:50am -07] wrote:
> I'm writing on behalf of a group of Emacs developers and users who share
> concerns about the future of the project. As we see other projects
> adopting policies which accept LLM contributions, it's encouraged us to
> see that the current Emacs policy disallows them.
>
> Our concerns stem from the temporary nature of the current policy, and its
> apparent focus on resolving legal uncertainty surrounding the copyright
> status of LLM-generated source code. Ensuring that the copyright of Emacs is
> legally defensible is of course important, but the way it appears to form the
> crux of the decision overlooks the harm posed to users and communities by
> acceptance of LLM-generated code. It would be a mistake to say that because
> something is legally allowable it must be acceptable, and for many of us who
> are deeply invested in Emacs, it's not a mistake we'd expect from this
> project's leadership.
>
> Free Software is more than just code. It's built on a foundation of trust and
> solidarity. The greatest strength of Emacs is not its technology but the user
> base which shares and works together. As we watch this discussion play out in
> other communities which have not taken a strong stance, we've seen the
> corrosive effect that LLMs have on trust and solidarity as soon as they are
> allowed in. We still have the chance to avoid that in the Emacs community and
> learn from the mistakes of others!
>
> (At this point I should clarify that an argument about whether these harms
> are serious is not my interest; so if you are opening your MUA to compose a
> reply on these lines, please consider that we've heard all the arguments
> already and remain unmoved by them. We're not interested in arguments about
> how powerful they are, nor about how local models will some day address the
> problems, nor about how people can lie about the provenance of their
> patches; none of these are relevant. You're welcome to discuss this on a
> separate thread.)

If the GNU project concluded that LLMs can generate code to be released
under a copyleft license tomorrow, we wouldn't immediately have an
anything-goes policy.  That would be a recipe for contributor and
maintainer burnout.

Instead, we'll think about whether limits need to be imposed to keep our
mailing list and bug tracker manageable.  For example, one idea I have
seen which seems promising (speaking just for myself) is to require
contributors to ask for prior permission from the maintainers to use an
LLM to implement something.  That prevents people dumping huge,
unreviewable patches on each other.

However, it is not realistic to hope that we will outright ban LLM
contributions, unless the GNU project as a whole does so.
The GNU project is meant to be a place where people who are politically
and morally aligned on the importance of free software, but not
necessarily on other matters, are able to work together.  That includes
people who think LLMs are awful and people who think they're great.

-- 
Sean Whitton

  > > "TeXlike-line-break" is short for "line breaking like the way TeX does
  > > it."  Either of those names describes this package's purpose clearly
  > > to anyone who has a basic awareness of what TeX does and what makes it
  > > so good.  That is a large fraction of our community.
  > >
  > > "TeX-fill" or "TeXlike-fill" would also achieve this.

  > May I ask to use other prefix than TeX?  This is the namespace of tex.el
  > (part of AUCTeX) which uses this as distinction to tex namespace in
  > tex-mode.el (part of Emacs).

Lower case, as in tex-fill or texlike-fill, would be equally good for
communicating that this package does line filling the way TeX does.

> +;;; utility functions
> +
> +(defun wait-for-coq ()
> +  "Wait until processing is complete."
> +  (while (or proof-second-action-list-active
> +             (consp proof-action-list))
> +    (accept-process-output nil 0.1)))
> +
> +(defun type-dot ()
> +  "Type \".\" in the current buffer, as if with the keyboard."
> +  (let ((last-command-event ?.))
> +    (call-interactively (key-binding "."))))
> +
> +(defmacro with-double-hit-file (&rest body)
> +  "Run BODY in a buffer visiting a Coq file ending with a sentence
> +without its final dot, at the end of the buffer."
> +  (declare (indent 0) (debug t))
> +  `(let ((file (make-temp-file "double_hit" nil ".v"
> +                               "Definition x := 0.\nDefinition y := 1"))
> +         buffer)
> +     (unwind-protect
> +         (progn
> +           (find-file file)
> +           (setq buffer (current-buffer))
> +           (goto-char (point-max))
> +           ,@body)
> +       (when buffer
> +         (with-current-buffer buffer
> +           (set-buffer-modified-p nil))
> +         (kill-buffer buffer))
> +       (when (proof-shell-live-buffer)
> +         (proof-shell-exit t))
> +       (delete-file file))))

total number is too high.

Or read-multiple-choice--short-answers itself could do that, in its use 
of x-popup-dialog.

Maybe that is what you meant by submenu? Otherwise, read-multiple-choice 
couldn't use the graphical prompt, IIUC.

> OTOH, it's possible to avoid a long list of menu items
> by removing kinds that provide no results when clicked
> like I did in the context menu.

That only works when we have a specific symbol at point we'll search 
for, I think. Whereas we aim to support completion of any symbol 
belonging to the specified kind. That requires broader scanning.

On 10/31/2026 12:27 AM, Eli Zaretskii wrote:
> And I find it futile to have any discussions about this initiative on
> this list anyway, since initiative's proponents made it clear there
> will be no discussion.  So, everybody, let's not waste any more energy
> and bandwidth on this.

I'm also a signatory on Human Emacs. For my part, I don't want to block 
all discussion, though I'd rather avoid rehashing introductory 
explanations about common ethical concerns. People can easily find these 
elsewhere online to learn about them, even if they don't end up agreeing.

I hope that forking won't be necessary, because I think the most 
valuable thing Emacs (and GNU and the FSF as a whole) has is its 
community. Splitting that community would be a tragedy. However, I also 
strongly believe that a permissive stance towards LLM-assisted 
contributions will damage this very same community even more profoundly 
in the long run. I hope to do what I can to preserve it.

Research has repeatedly shown that using LLMs to generate the results 
that someone wants will undermine their ability to learn new things, 
depriving themselves and the larger community from this hard-won 
knowledge. This is even more problematic for novices, who may have never 
had an opportunity to learn things the "old-fashioned" way. Worse still, 
the LLM providers advertise their models as equivalent to a junior 
engineer, encouraging maintainers to forego the work of mentoring novice 
contributors; even the Linux kernel has now decided to abandon 
mentorship, arguing that you must already be an expert or be prepared to 
spend lots of money on LLM tokens.

Working with LLMs also encourages isolation. According to the LLM 
providers, we should each be "orchestrating" a team of "agents" to do 
all our work; if there's a more socially-atomized way of producing 
software, I can't imagine what it is. Even LLM proponents I know have 
raised this concern, but have been unable to offer a solution. This 
echoes Mark Zuckerberg's assertion that people would like more friends, 
so his company will happily provide that in the form of chatbot "partners".

I've seen these problems play out in projects I'm involved with, 
projects I rely on, and projects I only hear about when the problems 
boil over. I've thus concluded that LLMs corrode the community practices 
that free software relies on. I'd hate to see Emacs, one of the projects 
that best embodies the vision of a different way of computing, to suffer 
the same fate.

No. I'm talking the huge economic interests (and open bets!) pushing this

On Thu, 29 Oct 2026, Stefan Monnier via "Emacs development discussions." wrote:

>> - M-<left>/M-<right> are taken for changing levels -- if you have sexp
>>   navigation on these keys, you get unintended edits of text blocks, and
>>   therefore much frustration.
>
> I guess the author took that behavior from `org-mode`.
> `outline-mode` uses `C-c C-<` and `C-c C->` instead for those operations.

Noting also that the major mode map takes precedence over the global
map, and therefore adding something to a major mode map which users are
likely to use as a global keybinding (such as M-<left>/M-<right> for
sexp navigation, windmove, or whatever else) forces users to learn about
intricacies of keymap precedence.  It's confusing for the uninitiated.

(vtable annoyingly takes over M-<left>/M-<right> as well, which I think
should be changed.)

Dear Jim Porter,

You say a permissive LLM policy would damage the community more than a
fork would.

Where's the evidence for that comparison, and who elected you to make
that trade-off for everyone else?

On 2026-11-03 08:27, Jim Porter wrote:
> Worse still, the LLM providers advertise their models as equivalent
> to a junior engineer, encouraging > maintainers to forego the work
> of mentoring novice contributors; even the Linux kernel has now
> decided to abandon mentorship, arguing that you must already be an
> expert or be prepared to spend lots of money on LLM tokens.

It is obvious here that you are conflating the term LLM and the
service as a software substitute API access which may or may not use
the LLM, plus many other services behind.

The term LLM means parametric function or weights as a file stored on
the file system, and trained using the language.

The "LLM provider" is not the LLM, you can only assume that such uses
LLM but cannot prove which technology behind actually generates
text. For example the new technology NCM is known as the LLM killer,
as it is not the LLM (I may be wrong) and user of service as a
software substitute APIs (those you call LLM providers) cannot know if
LLM is behind or NCM or some third different text generator.

So conflating those things isn't good neither for those in "Human"
Emacs group, or for any other user in the world.

So it is questionable:

- do you wish to say you are against the SaaSS API providers, which
   you call LLM providers?

- or are you against both the SaaSS API providers and the locally
   running LLMs?

- or are you against both?

- or are you also against the NCM and any other generative text
   technology that will come in future and which is not the LLM?

- LLMs are also used for embeddings, classification, decisions, not
   necessarily for text generation, so is the "Human" Emacs against the
   embeddings, classification, or decision making too?

- are you againt all machine learning systems? Like image generation
   too? Can a developer use the image generation system to generate
   images for Emacs? Or you will oppose it too?

- or you are against agent orchestration?

What exactly are you against? And what is the evidence  of harms, and
why you think that it is legitimate to pressurize the GNU project with
whatever outside requests, not discussed within the GNU project?

I am curious. I am not against your views, I am trying to understand
your views in full.

Irony with meanings is that Emacs actually came from MIT Artificial
Intelligence Laboratory (MIT AI Lab). It is the first child of
artificial intelligence.

I wish to know real hearms, and now let us go back to those alleged
harms:

> Working with LLMs also encourages isolation.

I'm not sure what kind of isolation you mean — if you mean social
isolation, that's plausible. But research papers can no longer be
taken at face value, especially when LLM-assisted. If your claim rests
on recent papers of that kind, I don't think they're a sound basis for
GNU project policy.

GNU project has a basis in GNU free software philosophy.

Technologies like LLM, NCM and new machine learning systems are all
part of software.

GNU free software philosophy wants freedom in computing for worldwide
users.

As long as it is free software, I do not see why would GNU project
officially warn against using LLMs. This I mean beyond the Emacs.

> According to the LLM providers, we should each be "orchestrating" a
> team of "agents" to do all our work; if there's a more
> socially-atomized way of producing software, I can't imagine what it
> is. Even LLM proponents I know have raised this concern, but have
> been unable to offer a solution. This echoes Mark Zuckerberg's
> assertion that people would like more friends, so his company will
> happily provide that in the form of chatbot "partners".

So here again I can see you are conflating the Service as a Software
Substitute with the actual LLM inference on user's computer.

So it is totally unclear what you are against for!
--------------------------------------------------

See my questions above and try to define what is it exactly you are
opposing. Please understand, I cannot understand you.

The harm you said is that LLM is creating isolation, and now... when
comparing it to larval stage of learning programming, that learning
alone may also create isolation, I could say haven thanks I was
isolated back in time in Stuttgart, Germany, during the night,
learning all of the GNU/Linux and programming. I have kept as few
social interactions. And why would that be bad? I have now many
employees and many social interactions. So I personally know that
using computers will increase isolation. But don't see harm
there. Also I don't see why would anybody else dictate over other
people their non-isolation.

Fundamentally, imposing purity rules on other people is itself a
violation of freedom principles.

GNU Emacs is free and "Human" Emacs users have legal rights to make
the fork. So your freedom is already there.

So why do you think one group that wants to split off should get to
override the freedom of everyone else? I genuinely don't follow the
reasoning.

Does this behavior make the Human Emacs group more human?

The "Human" Emacs claims moral ground of "human crafting" but does 
"Human" Emacs actually occupy the grund? The fork threat alone undercuts 
the claim of being "human". If your position is that Emacs should be 
built by humans, in community, through mentorship and shared practice, 
then announcing "commit to our policy or we fork" is a power move, not a 
craft move.

There is nothing more "human" within the "Human" Emacs than what is in 
GNU.

If the goal is a more human Emacs, does demanding a permanent ban from 
volunteers actually get you there (to become more human)?

> I've seen these problems play out in projects I'm involved with,
> projects I rely on, and projects I only hear about when the problems
> boil over. I've thus concluded that LLMs corrode the community
> practices that free software relies on. I'd hate to see Emacs, one
> of the projects that best embodies the vision of a different way of
> computing, to suffer the same fate.

So you really want to kill the wildebeest, put it to sleep, as not to 
suffer?

 From the outside, it looks like the plan is to put GNU Emacs to sleep
— like an animal spared further suffering — so that the purist and 
humanist
"Human" Emacs fork can carry on. I would rather understand your actual 
intent
than assume that.

-- 
Jean Louis

Reference:
https://www.gnu.org/philosophy/who-does-that-server-really-serve.html

On 2026-11-03 09:43, tomas@tuxteam.de wrote:
> On Mon, Nov 02, 2026 at 09:27:16PM -0800, Jim Porter wrote:

> 
>> Working with LLMs also encourages isolation [...]
> 
> Thanks, Jim. That has come to be the one thing I'd keep if I'd have
> to choose. If I'm in for something in Free Software it's for the
> community. And LLMs (LLMs-the business-model) are out to destroy
> exactly this.

Tomas, you wrote "LLMs (LLMs-the business-model)" — I want to pin that 
down, because the distinction matters. I download free GGUF models and 
run them locally; that's probably not a business model in your sense and 
doesn't involve any service (even though I use it for business). Are you 
opposing that too, or only the hosted commercial services? If it's only 
the services, then say so — because "LLMs" as a category includes things 
that raise none of the concerns you're describing. If it's the local 
models too, I'd like to understand why.

The files of a sample LLM are here: 
https://huggingface.co/LiquidAI/LFM3-1.7B/tree/main

So are you talking about files and using them in general? Is it wrong 
downloading free software licensed LLMs?

Or you are opposing remote service as a software substitute?

All of them?

Are you maybe conflating the LLM as parametric function, as weights 
calculated and stored as a file on the file system with the service as a 
software substitute as remote API access?

Another issue is LLMs are also used for decision making, classification, 
re-ranking. So it becomes unclear which LLM type you are talking about.

-- 
Jean Louis

> > +  "Type \".\" in the current buffer, as if with the keyboard."

> > +
> > +(defmacro with-double-hit-file (&rest body)

 <mailto:emacs-devel-request@gnu.org?subject=subscribe>
X-List-Received-Date: Tue, 03 Nov 2026 15:19:46 -0000

> Date: Mon, 2 Nov 2026 21:27:16 -0800
> Cc: phil@hagelb.org, emacs-devel@gnu.org
> From: Jim Porter <jporterbugs@gmail.com>
> 
> On 10/31/2026 12:27 AM, Eli Zaretskii wrote:
> > And I find it futile to have any discussions about this initiative on
> > this list anyway, since initiative's proponents made it clear there
> > will be no discussion.  So, everybody, let's not waste any more energy
> > and bandwidth on this.
> 
> I'm also a signatory on Human Emacs. For my part, I don't want to block 
> all discussion, though I'd rather avoid rehashing introductory 
> explanations about common ethical concerns. People can easily find these 
> elsewhere online to learn about them, even if they don't end up agreeing.

Thanks for describing your rationale.

> Research has repeatedly shown that using LLMs to generate the results 
> that someone wants will undermine their ability to learn new things, 
> depriving themselves and the larger community from this hard-won 
> knowledge. This is even more problematic for novices, who may have never 
> had an opportunity to learn things the "old-fashioned" way.

If some contributor decides to use LLMs for developing Emacs code, and
they do it in a way that prevents them from learning, that is their
business: the harm, such as it is, will be caused to them and no one
else.  Our job is to make sure that code that lands in Emacs is
useful, correct, well-designed and well-implemented.  We cannot be
held responsible for whatever the contributors do in the privacy of
their development environment.  I also think it's naïve, to say the
least, to think that by rejecting LLM-generated code we will affect
their decision to use LLM.

> Worse still, the LLM providers advertise their models as equivalent
> to a junior engineer, encouraging maintainers to forego the work of
> mentoring novice contributors; even the Linux kernel has now decided
> to abandon mentorship, arguing that you must already be an expert or
> be prepared to spend lots of money on LLM tokens.

We don't require knowledgeable Emacs developers to provide mentoring.
I personally think we should try (and do try myself), but this is not
a requirement.  It is subject to the decisions and free time resources
of each one of us.  We also have Real Lives, and frequently we see
someone who was very active in our forums to become much less active
or even disappear for prolonged periods of time; that also affects
mentoring.  Last, but not least, not every contributor reacts nicely
to mentoring; 'nough said.

All this happens _today_.  The result is that the level of mentoring
we provide is inconsistent and extremely variable.  And we live with
that, because frankly, what else could we do?

So I don't see why the (so far largely theoretical) danger that
someone will mentor less contributors who use LLMs is so fundamentally
different from them deciding to mentor less because they have less
free time or for whatever other reason.

And again, at least for me, the desire to mentor depends heavily on
the attitude of the mentoree -- if I see them eager to learn, I will
try harder, and if I see they don't like to be taught or don't want to
learn, I will stop.  It doesn't matter for me why they stop -- it's
their funeral, not mine and not Emacs's.  People should be free to do
what they feel is right for them, even if I think they are mistaken.

> Working with LLMs also encourages isolation. According to the LLM 
> providers, we should each be "orchestrating" a team of "agents" to do 
> all our work; if there's a more socially-atomized way of producing 
> software, I can't imagine what it is. Even LLM proponents I know have 
> raised this concern, but have been unable to offer a solution. This 
> echoes Mark Zuckerberg's assertion that people would like more friends, 
> so his company will happily provide that in the form of chatbot "partners".

Again, that's not our concern.  And again, it would be naïve to assume
that rejecting LLM-generated code will cause people become more
socialized.  Because if they want to use LLMs for Emacs, they probably
do that in umpteen other places, and if that makes them more
"isolated", nothing we can do or say is likely to change that.

> I've seen these problems play out in projects I'm involved with, 
> projects I rely on, and projects I only hear about when the problems 
> boil over. I've thus concluded that LLMs corrode the community practices 
> that free software relies on. I'd hate to see Emacs, one of the projects 
> that best embodies the vision of a different way of computing, to suffer 
> the same fate.

Please at least wait until there's some sign of something like that
happening in Emacs.  It is at least unfair to threaten a fork (which
110% sure is a blow to the community -- I'm old enough to remember how
things were during the XEmacs schism) just because the Linux kernel
did something we don't like or don't want to happen in our community.

Bottom line, I think these concerns are very premature, and in most
aspects simply not relevant to Emacs development.  To me, the actual
danger is the threat to fork as a kind of "precaution" against
problems that either didn't yet happen and are extremely unlikely to
happen, or are happening already due to a variety of real-life
reasons without causing any fuss.

And I'm most alarmed by the suggestion that we should reject someone's
code based on something that is unrelated to the code itself.  This is
a step along a slippery slope that I very much hope we will never
take.  Because the next thing we will be requested is to reject code
of people with particular views or opinions, or people living under
certain regimes, etc. etc.  We never did anything like that, and
IMNSHO we shouldn't, ever.  The world out there is full of grave
problems that need to be solved, but Emacs development is not the
right place to solve almost all of these problems.

point anymore.

Eli Zaretskii <eliz@gnu.org> writes:
> Please at least wait until there's some sign of something like that
> happening in Emacs.

How the discussions went about whether packages that steer people into /
suggest using / (insert other wording) proprietary services was such a
sign for me.

> And I'm most alarmed by the suggestion that we should reject someone's
> code based on something that is unrelated to the code itself.=20=20

There is a difference in code: people who hand out LLM output do not
actually write the same code as those who don=E2=80=99t, even when they wor=
k on
the same codebase, because code that works well for LLMs is different
from code that works well for humans.

To me that does not feel like working on code together, but more like
someone submitting code generated from UML diagrams and developed via
these =E2=80=93 with comments that ease detangling, but without actually
including the original source (the diagrams).

And expecting others to read the generated code, but as reply to reviews
adjusting the UML diagrams and regenerating the code.
And it=E2=80=99s exhausting to ask them for readability improvements (just =
to
see them ignored the next time again, because they don=E2=80=99t read the c=
ode).


At that point (with code generated from UML) I=E2=80=99d see three main opt=
ions:

=2D commit the original source (the diagrams) and accept that parts of the
  projects require understanding one more language to contribute. The
  source is then no longer the source of truth.

=2D accept that the original source is not included and decide what to do
  with the detangling comments (others can=E2=80=99t maintain them reliably=
).

=2D reject the contribution.


But the first isn=E2=80=99t actually possible with LLM output, because prom=
pts
and plans don=E2=80=99t work the same in different models or even versions =
of
the same models, and they aren=E2=80=99t meant for humans (else they wouldn=
=E2=80=99t
contain the phrases needed only by the LLM) and also not for being run
multiple times.

When collaborating with LLM-users at work I=E2=80=99ve already had to maint=
ain
other people=E2=80=99s AGENTS.md file and I=E2=80=99ve been directed to rea=
d long-winded
architecture files that are a kind of code but not really and require
knowledge of what the LLM of the current quarter makes of them to
understand what they mean.

And even more than with UML generated code, there=E2=80=99s a power disbala=
nce:
they expect me to read their generated code to work on the project, but
they only see my code through the lens of their LLM.
Not like auto-complete with a rendered docstring, but rather like asking
=E2=80=9Cwhat does this file do?=E2=80=9D.

How deep that goes differs by person: I=E2=80=99ve seen a continuum between
caring for the code itself and not reading any code, but there are only
two people of whom I know that they started using LLMs and still managed
not to move towards not caring how the actual code reads for humans.

The extreme end is that someone tells you that their code (they don=E2=80=
=99t
understand) is better than yours because they have 10 times as many
tests (they didn=E2=80=99t read).
And expecting you to replace your code with theirs due to that or to
explain why you don=E2=80=99t after being the first who read it =E2=80=93 a=
nd then to
argue with them because they trust the LLM more than you.

Therefore I think that opening the door to LLM-generated code only a
little bit is a path that leads to non-LLM users being pushed out of the
project.

> Because the next thing we will be requested is to reject code
> of people with particular views or opinions, or people living under
> certain regimes, etc. etc.  We never did anything like that, and
> IMNSHO we shouldn't, ever.

I intended not to write in this thread again, but I very much agree with
this point.

That=E2=80=99s why I decided to explain why I think that it does not apply =
to
contributing LLM output.

And I=E2=80=99m sorry that I couldn=E2=80=99t make that shorter.


Best wishes,
Arne
=2D-=20
Unpolitisch sein
hei=C3=9Ft politisch sein,
ohne es zu merken.
https://www.draketo.de

> From: "Dr. Arne Babenhauserheide" <arne_bab@web.de>
> Cc: Jim Porter <jporterbugs@gmail.com>,  bugs@gnu.support,  phil@hagelb.org,

> Eli Zaretskii <eliz@gnu.org> writes:
> > Please at least wait until there's some sign of something like that
> > happening in Emacs.
> 
> How the discussions went about whether packages that steer people into /
> suggest using / (insert other wording) proprietary services was such a
> sign for me.

I don't see the relevance, sorry.  That was a completely unrelated
issue.  Moreover, it is not yet over, so drawing any conclusions even
from that is premature.

> > And I'm most alarmed by the suggestion that we should reject someone's
> > code based on something that is unrelated to the code itself.  
> 
> There is a difference in code: people who hand out LLM output do not
> actually write the same code as those who don’t, even when they work on
> the same codebase, because code that works well for LLMs is different
> from code that works well for humans.

It is our job as Emacs maintainers to make sure that code which lands
in Emacs is written well and tested as thorough as possible.  There's
enough situations where novice contributors come up with patches that
need to be redesigned and/or reimplemented because they don't stand to
our standards, and it is our job to make sure they are improved until
they do.  And we do this job every day, as you can see here and on the
bug list.  Readability of the code and elegance of the implementation
are just two of the aspects we pay attention to, and will continue to
do so, as that is part of our job.

> And expecting others to read the generated code, but as reply to reviews
> adjusting the UML diagrams and regenerating the code.
> And it’s exhausting to ask them for readability improvements (just to
> see them ignored the next time again, because they don’t read the code).

Once again, this is part of our job.  If you think we don't do it well
enough, please show examples and we will work as hard as needed to
improve.

> At that point (with code generated from UML) I’d see three main options:
> 
> - commit the original source (the diagrams) and accept that parts of the
>   projects require understanding one more language to contribute. The
>   source is then no longer the source of truth.
> 
> - accept that the original source is not included and decide what to do
>   with the detangling comments (others can’t maintain them reliably).
> 
> - reject the contribution.
> 
> 
> But the first isn’t actually possible with LLM output, because prompts
> and plans don’t work the same in different models or even versions of
> the same models, and they aren’t meant for humans (else they wouldn’t
> contain the phrases needed only by the LLM) and also not for being run
> multiple times.
> 
> When collaborating with LLM-users at work I’ve already had to maintain
> other people’s AGENTS.md file and I’ve been directed to read long-winded
> architecture files that are a kind of code but not really and require
> knowledge of what the LLM of the current quarter makes of them to
> understand what they mean.

Here you seem to be assuming some specific conclusions that the GNU
Project will draw from their discussions of the legal aspects of
LLM-generated code, like the need to include the prompts and the
instructions to the model with the code.  This is again premature, and
we should delay these discussions until we have their decisions and
understand their practical implications.

This is definitely not something that could justify a fork, or even a
discussion of its possibility.

> The extreme end is that someone tells you that their code (they don’t
> understand) is better than yours because they have 10 times as many
> tests (they didn’t read).
> And expecting you to replace your code with theirs due to that or to
> explain why you don’t after being the first who read it – and then to
> argue with them because they trust the LLM more than you.
> 
> Therefore I think that opening the door to LLM-generated code only a
> little bit is a path that leads to non-LLM users being pushed out of the
> project.

That will not happen unless the then Emacs maintainers decide to do
that (and I don't see why they would).  If that becomes a possibility,
these considerations should be raised then.  Not now, when this is a
distant potential danger, IMO largely academic.  Personally, I would
be very surprised if any Emacs maintainer in their right mind would
ever agree to something like that.  We are not different from you in
these matters and our experiences with managing software projects are
also similar.  I don't understand why you and others have such fears
at all.  Would you have such fears if you were the Emacs maintainer?
I guess not.

Eli Zaretskii <eliz@gnu.org> writes:

[...]

> And I'm most alarmed by the suggestion that we should reject someone's
> code based on something that is unrelated to the code itself.  This is
> a step along a slippery slope that I very much hope we will never
> take.  Because the next thing we will be requested is to reject code
> of people with particular views or opinions, or people living under
> certain regimes, etc. etc.  We never did anything like that, and
> IMNSHO we shouldn't, ever.  The world out there is full of grave
> problems that need to be solved, but Emacs development is not the
> right place to solve almost all of these problems.

<perhaps-a-tangent>
Then again, we have rejected patches because the author hasn't signed
the FSF copyright assignment -- a practice that some other GNU projects
have dropped, such as GCC: https://lwn.net/Articles/857791/.
</perhaps-a-tangent>

Eli Zaretskii <eliz@gnu.org> writes:

> It is our job as Emacs maintainers to make sure that code which lands
> in Emacs is written well and tested as thorough as possible.

This is not the point.

The point is that producing LLM output uses a different language than
writing code. So it effectively adds a language to the project.

Not just some language (which would already be risky), but a language
that does not have a corresponding source that can reproduce its output
(except if you include the whole model as well as the seed and the
complete context along with the prompt, of which at least model and seed
are not available for the big models and the model is not Free Software).

> Would you have such fears if you were the Emacs maintainer?

Yes, I would.

Otherwise I would not write them, because while I think you=E2=80=99re wron=
g on
this point, I you and the work you do as maintainer have my highest
respect.

Best wishes,
Arne
=2D-=20
Unpolitisch sein
hei=C3=9Ft politisch sein,
ohne es zu merken.
https://www.draketo.de

You will need to explain that, because I really don't understand what
you are trying to say.  The generated code uses the programming
language, be it C or Lisp, in which Emacs is written.

> Not just some language (which would already be risky), but a language
> that does not have a corresponding source that can reproduce its output
> (except if you include the whole model as well as the seed and the
> complete context along with the prompt, of which at least model and seed
> are not available for the big models and the model is not Free Software).

Onmce again, what needs to be documented and recorded as part of
admitting such contributions is a separate issue.  If it will be
decided that the prompts etc. need to be added, we will need to learn
how to review them.  But since we already review similar assets, I see
no reason to be afraid here.

> > Would you have such fears if you were the Emacs maintainer?
> 
> Yes, I would.

I'm surprised, but my faith in the abilities of the Emacs maintainers
is high enough to not have such fears.

It is worth a try.

On 03/11/2026 05:11, Stefan Monnier via Emacs development discussions. 
wrote:
>> If this restriction can't be lifted, then maybe read-multiple-choice
>> could restructure the long menu by leaving only 8 items, and
>> moving the rest into a new submenu.
> 
> But I can't see anything in the calling convention of
> `read-multiple-choice` that would let it guess which items are more
> deserving to be in the main dialog, so it risks being occasionally
> rather awkward with unlikely choices being (unluckily) presented first
> and the most common ones relegated to a "new submenu"?
I think the items that are returned closer to the front of the list get 
the more prioritized treatment and better visibility anyway.

The result is not sorted, so it's up to the backend to choose which 
kinds are printed first. Those in the tail of the list could be cordoned 
off in "Others" or behind paging, etc.

As long as the "soft max" is stable, the backend authors can test the 
result and structure their lists accordingly.

On Wed, Nov 04, 2026 at 05:32:23AM +0200, Eli Zaretskii wrote:
> > From: "Dr. Arne Babenhauserheide" <arne_bab@web.de>
> > Cc: jporterbugs@gmail.com,  bugs@gnu.support,  phil@hagelb.org,
> >   emacs-devel@gnu.org
> > Date: Tue, 03 Nov 2026 22:59:49 +0100
> >=20
> > Eli Zaretskii <eliz@gnu.org> writes:
> >=20
> > > It is our job as Emacs maintainers to make sure that code which lands
> > > in Emacs is written well and tested as thorough as possible.
> >=20
> > This is not the point.
> >=20
> > The point is that producing LLM output uses a different language than
> > writing code. So it effectively adds a language to the project.
>=20
> You will need to explain that, because I really don't understand what
> you are trying to say.  The generated code uses the programming
> language, be it C or Lisp, in which Emacs is written.

If I may chime in here, this is what GNU calls the "preferred form".
If the C, or the Lisp is just the output of a process (a generatively
operated LLM, with some random input [aka "temperature"]), this
"preferred source" would be the model plus the random seed, not its
output.

Exactly as if some C code had been generated by Lex/Yacc (or, better:
Bison, but we're biased here ;-)

Cheers
--=20
t

Eli Zaretskii <eliz@gnu.org> writes:

>> The point is that producing LLM output uses a different language than
>> writing code. So it effectively adds a language to the project.
>
> You will need to explain that, because I really don't understand what
> you are trying to say.  The generated code uses the programming
> language, be it C or Lisp, in which Emacs is written.

That=E2=80=99s what I tried to do with the UML analogy: when you generate c=
ode
from an UML diagram, you do not actually write in the language of the
code. You write UML.

Similarly when you write prompts, you do not actually write the code,
but you write instructions that say something about the code, but to get
them right, you have to have a feeling for the representation the LLM
has of the code (which you can=E2=80=99t actually know).

Depending on the usage style, this can be closer to or further away from
the actual code, but in any way it=E2=80=99s not actually the code. And it=
=E2=80=99s
very different from refactoring tools with which you do precise
manipulations of the code on the level of the code.

An easier to convey example is with AI-art: if you tell an AI model
=E2=80=9Cmake this blue=E2=80=9D, it often doesn=E2=80=99t only make this b=
lue, but adds other
changes. An art director explained a while back that this makes it
almost impossible for him to work with people who use those tools. While
the results seem similar, they don=E2=80=99t actually use the same language=
 to
produce them.

And that creates a constant pressure to just accept what the model
outputs today (besides anchoring effects that make this stronger),
detaching the user from the actual code. Example quote (I heard multiple
times): =E2=80=9Cnot exactly what I wanted, but doesn=E2=80=99t do harm, ri=
ght?=E2=80=9D.

It also causes pressure to look more at things the tool can check (e.g.
=E2=80=9Cthe tests are green=E2=80=9D or =E2=80=9CI can click through=E2=80=
=9D) and less on the code.
But those are indicators that help catch common errors and not the
source of truth for correctness (except for programs like sqlite that
have massive testsuites and are exposed to every strange bug under the
sun).

>> Not just some language (which would already be risky), but a language
>> that does not have a corresponding source that can reproduce its output

> Once again, what needs to be documented and recorded as part of
> admitting such contributions is a separate issue.

It=E2=80=99s not about what to admit: it=E2=80=99s about whether it would a=
ctually be
possible to just say the equivalent of =E2=80=9Clet the source of this part=
 be
the UML diagram / XML definition / =E2=80=A6=E2=80=9D.

Best wishes,
Arne
=2D-=20
Unpolitisch sein
hei=C3=9Ft politisch sein,
ohne es zu merken.
https://www.draketo.de

Jean Louis <bugs@gnu.support> writes:

> On 2026-11-04 10:08, Gerd M=C3=B6llmann wrote:
>
>> ...and the difficulty that LLM output is non-determinitic
>
> From view point of running local the local LLM inference, the above
> statement is only true when user sets the temperature to something
> else then zero. In GNU project we should never discuss running the
> remote SaaSS API but local computing.
>
> If temperature is zero, the output is deterministic and reproducible.
> Of course other parameters should be made known as well to get full
> reproducibility.
>
> Example:
>
> llama-cli --temp 0 -m
> /mnt/nvme0n1/LLM/quantized/Qwen3.5-9B-Uncensored-HauhauCS-Aggressive-Q4_K=
_M.gguf
> --prompt "Who is RMS aka Stallman?"
>
> Try it out. It gives deterministic output each single time. The above
> prompt will always give totally same output, as the model picks the
> single most probable token at every step.
>
> It is reproducible. It is alright to use temperature zero in coding,
> especially in debugging and testing, it is even faster slightly.
>
> In an LLM-allowed programming environment, programmers could use a
> shared model (with same checksum) to collaborate deterministically,
> generating Emacs lisp functions sequentially while maintaining full
> understanding of each function and ensuring a coherent workflow.
>
> Thus, the problems with foundation of not being deterministic can be
> resolved by establishing a deterministic, local inference workflow
> using a shared same model with a fixed checksum.

Maybe, I'm not an expert in this stuff. But I must say that I've more
than once seen articles claiming that temperature 0 is not enough, for
example
:
https://iamvera.ai/blog/temperature-zero-reproducible-code-generation/

Hello Emacs Devel,

Finally, we have released v0.4.0 of EMuReader. It is now ready to be packaged for GNU ELPA. All the renaming, building and other issues have been addressed.

The release notes can be found here:

https://codeberg.org/MonadicSheep/emureader/releases/tag/0.4.0

or, the following NEWS file entry:

* Version 0.4.0
- Rename to EMuReader.
- Per window pages, zooming, etc. support, which means that it is now
  possible to view different pages of the same document (from the same
  buffer) on different windows.
- Change 'screenful' to 'screen' in command names.
- Make =emureader-reset-size= interactive.
- Overall stability improvements when opening files, accessing
  bookmarks, and outlines.
- Remove F5 keybinding for =emureader-presentation-mode=.
- Remove =emureader-mode= as an interactive command.
- Fix weird page border artifacts when using the mouse.
- Add =emureader-build= command to automatically build the dynamic module.
- Fix handling documents with multiple orientations.
- EMuReader buffers now get automatically reverted via =auto-revert-mode= without crashing.

Thank you everyone for your inputs and cooperation.

Regards,
-- 
Divya Ranjan Pattanaik,
Philosophy, Mathematics, Libre Software.

PGP Fingerprint: F0B3 1A69 8006 8FB8 096A  2F12 B245 10C6 108C 8D4A

On 2026-11-04 10:08, Gerd Möllmann wrote:

> ...and the difficulty that LLM output is non-determinitic

 From view point of running local the local LLM inference, the above 
statement is only true when user sets the temperature to something else 
then zero. In GNU project we should never discuss running the remote 
SaaSS API but local computing.

If temperature is zero, the output is deterministic and reproducible. Of 
course other parameters should be made known as well to get full 
reproducibility.

Example:

llama-cli --temp 0 -m 
/mnt/nvme0n1/LLM/quantized/Qwen3.5-9B-Uncensored-HauhauCS-Aggressive-Q4_K_M.gguf 
--prompt "Who is RMS aka Stallman?"

Try it out. It gives deterministic output each single time. The above 
prompt will always give totally same output, as the model picks the 
single most probable token at every step.

It is reproducible. It is alright to use temperature zero in coding, 
especially in debugging and testing, it is even faster slightly.

In an LLM-allowed programming environment, programmers could use a 
shared model (with same checksum) to collaborate deterministically, 
generating Emacs lisp functions sequentially while maintaining full 
understanding of each function and ensuring a coherent workflow.

Thus, the problems with foundation of not being deterministic can be 
resolved by establishing a deterministic, local inference workflow using 
a shared same model with a fixed checksum.

-- 
Jean Louis

> If I may chime in here, this is what GNU calls the "preferred form".
> If the C, or the Lisp is just the output of a process (a generatively
> operated LLM, with some random input [aka "temperature"]), this
> "preferred source" would be the model plus the random seed, not its
> output.
> 
> Exactly as if some C code had been generated by Lex/Yacc (or, better:
> Bison, but we're biased here ;-)

So it''s again not relevant to the issue at hand.  We should all wait
until GNU decides whether LLM-generated code is acceptable, and if so,
in what form.  The latter guidance is obviously dependent on the
decision about the former and the conditions for accepting such code,
if possible, that will emerge from the legal and other aspects of
that.

It is useless to discuss this here and now, and arguing that the
(currently completely undefined) form of such code is a reason for a
threat to fork is...strange (for lack of a better and still polite
word).

> >> Not just some language (which would already be risky), but a language
> >> that does not have a corresponding source that can reproduce its output
> 
> > Once again, what needs to be documented and recorded as part of
> > admitting such contributions is a separate issue.
> 
> It’s not about what to admit: it’s about whether it would actually be
> possible to just say the equivalent of “let the source of this part be
> the UML diagram / XML definition / …”.

Leave this to us the Emacs maintainers, please, for when this is
clarified.  If and when GNU publishes the guidelines and requirements
for accepting LLM-generated code, if the decision is to allow that, we
will study what those guidelines and requirements mean in practice for
us, and will see how to modify our procedures if needed.  For now,
this side-bar discussion is completely academic, as we don't have any
real basis for assessing what that entails and what it will require
from us the maintainers.  We can argue till Kingdom Come and not make
any progress, because we don't really know what are we arguing about.

>

Great, I have added the package to gnu.git and it should appear there
shorty.

> or, the following NEWS file entry:
>
> * Version 0.4.0
> - Rename to EMuReader.
> - Per window pages, zooming, etc. support, which means that it is now
>   possible to view different pages of the same document (from the same
>   buffer) on different windows.
> - Change 'screenful' to 'screen' in command names.
> - Make =emureader-reset-size= interactive.
> - Overall stability improvements when opening files, accessing
>   bookmarks, and outlines.
> - Remove F5 keybinding for =emureader-presentation-mode=.
> - Remove =emureader-mode= as an interactive command.
> - Fix weird page border artifacts when using the mouse.
> - Add =emureader-build= command to automatically build the dynamic module.
> - Fix handling documents with multiple orientations.
> - EMuReader buffers now get automatically reverted via =auto-revert-mode= without crashing.
>
> Thank you everyone for your inputs and cooperation.

Thank you for you and your collaborators for the submission and your
patience.

> Regards,

Eli Zaretskii <eliz@gnu.org> writes:

> If some contributor decides to use LLMs for developing Emacs code,
> and they do it in a way that prevents them from learning, that is
> their business: the harm, such as it is, will be caused to them and
> no one else.

> Our job is to make sure that code that lands in Emacs is useful,
> correct, well-designed and well-implemented.  We cannot be held
> responsible for whatever the contributors do in the privacy of their
> development environment.

There's one issue with that - after such code gets merged, it can
become your business.

Say, someone made a contribution to Emacs, a particularly large one.
They spent tons of tokens on it, made tests and all, and the code
looks solid.  Maintainers do the review, ask for some changes.  The
user spends more tokens, changes the code, and eventually it is
decided that all is good and the code is fine to merge to Emacs.

That person doesn't understand the code they made, because it was done
by an LLM.  Sure it's their problem.  But once it gets merged, it is
now problem for the maintainers.  The contributor walks away, and if
in the future any issues arise, that person may just not respond at
all.  Now someone else needs to go through all that code and figure
out the intentions that the LLM had while writing it.  This can get
out of hand pretty fast.

I sometimes submit bugs to Emacs, and I often see that someone who's
responsible to the changes that introduced these bugs are called, and
they come and fix these bugs, as any responsible contributor should.
Assuming that this will work the same with people who used an LLM to
make a contribution is problematic, and I've already encountered it in
the projects I maintain.

Sure, this can happen without LLMs involved - there are irresponsible
people who would walk away just the same.  But allowing the use of LLM
opens the door to getting much more of such contributions, and can quickly
overwhelm a small team with amounts of code they now need to maintain
without help.

-- 
Andrey Listopadov

> Eli Zaretskii <eliz@gnu.org> writes:
> 
> > If some contributor decides to use LLMs for developing Emacs code,
> > and they do it in a way that prevents them from learning, that is
> > their business: the harm, such as it is, will be caused to them and
> > no one else.
> 
> > Our job is to make sure that code that lands in Emacs is useful,
> > correct, well-designed and well-implemented.  We cannot be held
> > responsible for whatever the contributors do in the privacy of their
> > development environment.
> 
> There's one issue with that - after such code gets merged, it can
> become your business.

It happens today, quite frequently.  I see nothing new here.

> Say, someone made a contribution to Emacs, a particularly large one.
> They spent tons of tokens on it, made tests and all, and the code
> looks solid.  Maintainers do the review, ask for some changes.  The
> user spends more tokens, changes the code, and eventually it is
> decided that all is good and the code is fine to merge to Emacs.
> 
> That person doesn't understand the code they made, because it was done
> by an LLM.  Sure it's their problem.  But once it gets merged, it is
> now problem for the maintainers.  The contributor walks away, and if
> in the future any issues arise, that person may just not respond at
> all.  Now someone else needs to go through all that code and figure
> out the intentions that the LLM had while writing it.  This can get
> out of hand pretty fast.

As I said, we already do that, quite a lot.  People disappear from
Emacs, after contributing something, for any number of reasons, and
leave their code for us to develop and maintain.  I could show quite a
few examples of this over the years, some of them of significant
changes in Emacs.

And yes, sometimes it means we have code in Emacs that we are not
familiar with well enough, and need to tread carefully when we modify
it.  But it's part of our life in Emacs development, not something new
with LLMs.

> I sometimes submit bugs to Emacs, and I often see that someone who's
> responsible to the changes that introduced these bugs are called, and
> they come and fix these bugs, as any responsible contributor should.

IME, more often than not, the original authors are unavailable, for
whatever reasons.  The situation where they stay active for many years
is quite rare.

> Sure, this can happen without LLMs involved - there are irresponsible
> people who would walk away just the same.

This usually has nothing to do with being irresponsible.  There are
many reasons why the original authors could be unavailable.

> But allowing the use of LLM opens the door to getting much more of
> such contributions, and can quickly overwhelm a small team with
> amounts of code they now need to maintain without help.

As long as the code is well understood when it is accepted, and it's
clean and designed reasonably well, I don't see why this would be a
problem any more than what we have now.

I would like to submit the following package to GNU ELPA:

  https://codeberg.org/astoff/readline-completion.el

It provides a CAPF that reads completion candidates from GNU Readline.
Unlike some similar existing packages, it relies on a new(ish) readline
feature (M-x export-completions) that reduces the amount of guesswork
involved.

Apparently Spencer was involved in requesting/designing that GNU
Readline feature, so I've added you in CC.  Perhaps you have comments?

Hi,

I would like to submit the following package to Non-GNU ELPA:

  https://github.com/astoff/vibe-spell

It is a slop-rewrite of Hunspell/Nuspell in pure Elisp.  I think it
might be useful for some users, since currently the only options for
accessing a spellchecker involve compiling a third-party module (jinx)
or suffering with the "ispell -a" interface.

Also, I think this package is an interesting experiment in how far Elisp
can be pushed.  It performs better than I would have guessed.

Once this is online, I will update my jit-spell package to optionally
make use of vibe-spell (since the latter is just a library, without UI).

Something like the patch below?


=== Stefan


diff --git a/lisp/mwheel.el b/lisp/mwheel.el
index 47eb236c521..9acc2381e66 100644
--- a/lisp/mwheel.el
+++ b/lisp/mwheel.el
@@ -140,7 +140,20 @@ mouse-wheel-scroll-amount
   :set #'mouse-wheel-change-button
   :version "28.1")
 
-(defcustom mouse-wheel-progressive-speed t
+(defcustom mouse-wheel-progressive-speed
+  (cond
+   ((featurep 'cocoa)
+    ;; macOS-like defaults for trackpad and mouse wheel scrolling on
+    ;; macOS 10.7+.
+    (defvar ns-version-string)
+    (let ((appkit-version
+           (and (string-match "^appkit-\\([^\s-]*\\)" ns-version-string)
+                (string-to-number (match-string 1 ns-version-string)))))
+     ;; Appkit 1138 ~= macOS 10.7.
+      ;; macOS 10.7 is ancient.  We could just default this to nil for ns
+      ;; in the defcustom in mwheel.el.
+      (< appkit-version 1138)))
+   (t t))
   "If nil, scrolling speed is proportional to the wheel speed.
 If non-nil, moving the wheel faster will make scrolling
 progressively faster.

>     Restore current buffer on nonlocal exits
>     
>     * src/process.c (read_and_insert_process_output)
>     (Finternal_default_process_filter): Protect the current buffer
>     from changes by user hooks (bug#81677).

Hmm...

In the two places where you've put the
`record_unwind_current_buffer...unbind_to`, AFAICT the code in the `...`
is not expected to change current-buffer.  Instead, current-buffer is
set just a bit earlier by `read_process_output_before_insert`.

Any chance you meant to put the `record_unwind_current_buffer` *before*
the call to `read_process_output_before_insert` rather than after it?
If not, I think the code deserves extra comment to explain why.


=== Stefan

Augusto Stoffel <arstoffel@gmail.com> writes:

> Hi,
>
> I would like to submit the following package to Non-GNU ELPA:
>
>   https://github.com/astoff/vibe-spell
>
> It is a slop-rewrite of Hunspell/Nuspell in pure Elisp.  I think it
> might be useful for some users, since currently the only options for
> accessing a spellchecker involve compiling a third-party module (jinx)
> or suffering with the "ispell -a" interface.
>
> Also, I think this package is an interesting experiment in how far Elisp
> can be pushed.  It performs better than I would have guessed.
>
> Once this is online, I will update my jit-spell package to optionally
> make use of vibe-spell (since the latter is just a library, without UI).

If you wouldn't mind, I think it would be better to wait this one out
for a bit before adding the package.  There has been the suggestion to
wait at least a month after a LLM generated package has been written
before adding it to any archive, just to see how it pans out and if the
author is interested in maintaining it in the long term.  At almost 3000
lines of code, it is also a chunk to review.  Are you planning to
rewrite anything by hand (e.g. I notice that all docstrings are
comments)?  My recommendation would be to advertise the package in
communities online and see how people respond to it in day-to-day usage.

On Wed,  4 Nov 2026, Philip Kaludercic wrote:

> Augusto Stoffel <arstoffel@gmail.com> writes:
>
>> Hi,
>>
>> I would like to submit the following package to Non-GNU ELPA:
>>
>>   https://github.com/astoff/vibe-spell
>>
>> It is a slop-rewrite of Hunspell/Nuspell in pure Elisp.  I think it
>> might be useful for some users, since currently the only options for
>> accessing a spellchecker involve compiling a third-party module (jinx)
>> or suffering with the "ispell -a" interface.
>>
>> Also, I think this package is an interesting experiment in how far Elisp
>> can be pushed.  It performs better than I would have guessed.
>>
>> Once this is online, I will update my jit-spell package to optionally
>> make use of vibe-spell (since the latter is just a library, without UI).
>
> If you wouldn't mind, I think it would be better to wait this one out
> for a bit before adding the package.  There has been the suggestion to
> wait at least a month after a LLM generated package has been written
> before adding it to any archive, just to see how it pans out and if the
> author is interested in maintaining it in the long term.

I don't mind, but I'll mention I've been using this for about a month
myself, so I'd say the private stage of development has run its course.

> At almost 3000 lines of code, it is also a chunk to review.  Are you
> planning to rewrite anything by hand (e.g. I notice that all
> docstrings are comments)?

Here, for better or worse, the perspective must be a bit different.  I
didn't ask an LLM to help me write some code; I just made it translate
from C++ to Elisp, and keep iterating until the Hunspell tests pass and
the obvious performance bottlenecks are fixed.  The code in a way
doesn't matter, and I only looked cursorily at it.

That's also why I intentionally replaced the docstrings with comments,
so no user gets their eyes hurt with slop.

On the other hand, the docstrings, readme, etc. are not slop =F0=9F=99=82.

>  My recommendation would be to advertise the package in communities
> online and see how people respond to it in day-to-day usage.

Okay, maybe some people from this list get interested.

Augusto Stoffel <arstoffel@gmail.com> writes:

> On Wed,  4 Nov 2026, Philip Kaludercic wrote:
>
>> Augusto Stoffel <arstoffel@gmail.com> writes:
>>
>>> Hi,
>>>
>>> I would like to submit the following package to Non-GNU ELPA:
>>>
>>>   https://github.com/astoff/vibe-spell
>>>
>>> It is a slop-rewrite of Hunspell/Nuspell in pure Elisp.  I think it
>>> might be useful for some users, since currently the only options for
>>> accessing a spellchecker involve compiling a third-party module (jinx)
>>> or suffering with the "ispell -a" interface.
>>>
>>> Also, I think this package is an interesting experiment in how far Elisp
>>> can be pushed.  It performs better than I would have guessed.
>>>
>>> Once this is online, I will update my jit-spell package to optionally
>>> make use of vibe-spell (since the latter is just a library, without UI).
>>
>> If you wouldn't mind, I think it would be better to wait this one out
>> for a bit before adding the package.  There has been the suggestion to
>> wait at least a month after a LLM generated package has been written
>> before adding it to any archive, just to see how it pans out and if the
>> author is interested in maintaining it in the long term.
>
> I don't mind, but I'll mention I've been using this for about a month
> myself, so I'd say the private stage of development has run its course.

OK, that is a good sign.  What would you argue are the advantages of
this approach as opposed to using an existing program Hunspell/Nuspell?
I get that it is interesting, I am just skeptical of the belief that all
functionality has to be written in Emacs Lisp instead of integrating
existing and general tooling into Emacs (like what ispell does).

>> At almost 3000 lines of code, it is also a chunk to review.  Are you
>> planning to rewrite anything by hand (e.g. I notice that all
>> docstrings are comments)?
>
> Here, for better or worse, the perspective must be a bit different.  I
> didn't ask an LLM to help me write some code; I just made it translate
> from C++ to Elisp, and keep iterating until the Hunspell tests pass and
> the obvious performance bottlenecks are fixed.  The code in a way
> doesn't matter, and I only looked cursorily at it.
>
> That's also why I intentionally replaced the docstrings with comments,
> so no user gets their eyes hurt with slop.

So you would claim that vibe-spell.el is effectively not source code,
but just the output of a non-conventional compilation process from C++
to Elisp?

> On the other hand, the docstrings, readme, etc. are not slop =F0=9F=99=82.

I guess that makes sense, but it still feels weird to have so many
functions without any proper documentation.

>>  My recommendation would be to advertise the package in communities
>> online and see how people respond to it in day-to-day usage.
>
> Okay, maybe some people from this list get interested.

It might make sense to ping Sacha to link the repo on her Emacs News
Newsletter to attract more attention.

My main comment is that I'm very happy to see this package.  I never got
around to finishing the Emacs integration for this readline feature, but
the readline feature was added for this exact use case (supporting
completion in Emacs), so thank you for finishing it!

I only did a cursory skim, but I think you might be able to get remote
support for M-& completion by passing :file-handler to make-process.

> From: Philip Kaludercic <philipk@posteo.net>
> Cc: emacs-devel@gnu.org
> Date: Wed, 04 Nov 2026 20:43:11 +0000
> 
> Augusto Stoffel <arstoffel@gmail.com> writes:
> 
> > Here, for better or worse, the perspective must be a bit different.  I
> > didn't ask an LLM to help me write some code; I just made it translate
> > from C++ to Elisp, and keep iterating until the Hunspell tests pass and
> > the obvious performance bottlenecks are fixed.  The code in a way
> > doesn't matter, and I only looked cursorily at it.
> >
> > That's also why I intentionally replaced the docstrings with comments,
> > so no user gets their eyes hurt with slop.
> 
> So you would claim that vibe-spell.el is effectively not source code,
> but just the output of a non-conventional compilation process from C++
> to Elisp?

If this is the case, then IMO the package should include both the
original Hunspell code used for conversion and the prompts used to
generate the final ELisp version.  IOW, the "source code" here is some
specific version of Hunspell, which thjus should be in the package,
and the package should also include all the scripts and tools and
instructions needed to regenerate the ELisp version.  Imagine the
scenario where someone wants to cherry-pick some change in upstream
Hunspell to this package: they will need to patch the Hunspell sources
and then run the LLM to produce the corresponding ELisp changes.  They
will need everything that this process requires.

I believe this is a basic requirement of GPL, isn't it?

On Wed,  4 Nov 2026, Philip Kaludercic wrote:

> OK, that is a good sign.  What would you argue are the advantages of
> this approach as opposed to using an existing program Hunspell/Nuspell?
> I get that it is interesting, I am just skeptical of the belief that all
> functionality has to be written in Emacs Lisp instead of integrating
> existing and general tooling into Emacs (like what ispell does).

The problem with communicating with Hunspell (or any spellchecker) by
subprocesses via the standard "ispell -a" interface is that you cannot
opt out of computing correction suggestions for every incorrect word,
and that's _very_ slow (3 orders of magnitude slower than just
checking).

The idea of linking Emacs against some spellchecking library has
apparently been ruled out (judging by the fact that it never happened),
and an FFI is also not planned, if I understand correctly.

Then we could debate whether "all users are able to compile an Emacs
module" is a true and/or desirable fact.  But if we also exclude that
approach, then really I see no other option open for a good
spellchecking experience.

>>> At almost 3000 lines of code, it is also a chunk to review.  Are you
>>> planning to rewrite anything by hand (e.g. I notice that all
>>> docstrings are comments)?
>>
>> Here, for better or worse, the perspective must be a bit different.  I
>> didn't ask an LLM to help me write some code; I just made it translate
>> from C++ to Elisp, and keep iterating until the Hunspell tests pass and
>> the obvious performance bottlenecks are fixed.  The code in a way
>> doesn't matter, and I only looked cursorily at it.
>>
>> That's also why I intentionally replaced the docstrings with comments,
>> so no user gets their eyes hurt with slop.
>
> So you would claim that vibe-spell.el is effectively not source code,
> but just the output of a non-conventional compilation process from C++
> to Elisp?

Hum, I don't like the analogy with compilers.  That would force us to
conclude that the prompts I used to generate the .el files are "source
code", which clearly they are not...  It's really a rewrite, in my view.

>> On the other hand, the docstrings, readme, etc. are not slop =F0=9F=99=
=82.
>
> I guess that makes sense, but it still feels weird to have so many
> functions without any proper documentation.

Yes, me too, actually.  What changes things a bit for me is the fact
that we have a very small interface which is quite well specified in
terms of Hunspell's test suite.

>>>  My recommendation would be to advertise the package in communities
>>> online and see how people respond to it in day-to-day usage.
>>
>> Okay, maybe some people from this list get interested.
>
> It might make sense to ping Sacha to link the repo on her Emacs News
> Newsletter to attract more attention.

> On Wed,  4 Nov 2026, Philip Kaludercic wrote:

>> OK, that is a good sign.  What would you argue are the advantages of

>> I get that it is interesting, I am just skeptical of the belief that all
>> functionality has to be written in Emacs Lisp instead of integrating

>
> The problem with communicating with Hunspell (or any spellchecker) by
> subprocesses via the standard "ispell -a" interface is that you cannot
> opt out of computing correction suggestions for every incorrect word,
> and that's _very_ slow (3 orders of magnitude slower than just
> checking).

I am not familiar with the "ispell -a" interface, but do you know if
some other interface could also help solve the problem?

> The idea of linking Emacs against some spellchecking library has
> apparently been ruled out (judging by the fact that it never happened),
> and an FFI is also not planned, if I understand correctly.

Isn't that what Jinx does?

> Then we could debate whether "all users are able to compile an Emacs
> module" is a true and/or desirable fact.  But if we also exclude that
> approach, then really I see no other option open for a good
> spellchecking experience.

I see, thank you for elaborating the argument for your approach!

What I meant is that the prompt would generate the (throwaway)

files.

I guess the question practically boils down to whether or not you expect
people (including humans ... weird times) to modify the source code
themselves if they want to make changes or adopt upstream features or if

The UNIX way is to treat a new thing as a stranger: the standard doesn't
change because the face is familiar.

LLMs deserve the same. Not "welcome", not "ban", but review with the same
standard. If an LLM-generated patch passes review and the contributor can
answer questions about it, accept it. If not, reject it.

The maintenance-debt concern is real but not unique to LLMs. The problem
is in review and handover, not in the origin of the code.

JUN JIE NAN

The change of current-time-list broke my package rau:
https://github.com/thkoch2001/rau/issues/9

The break actually happens in the logging library lgr used in rau:
https://github.com/Fuco1/emacs-lgr/issues/3

I don't think there is anything reasonable for me to do in Rau but to hope that Matus will be so kind to fix this issue in lgr before the release of Emacs 32.

With all due respect, I wonder whether the way this change is done could not be improved. Maybe by introducing a new function and deprecating the old one?

Thank you for your work!

Thomas Koch

Hi,

I'd like to revisit the 2022 discussion on supporting existing Emacs
image specs on text terminals, starting with Kitty graphics.

In my `image-line` prototype, images are laid out as one-cell-high
rows. I verified in Kitty that the visible portion stays on screen
when an image is clipped at either vertical window edge. A separate
Canvas prototype updates pixels without moving the placement.

Would extending `insert-sliced-image` to a Kitty-capable TTY be a
reasonable first step?

Previous discussion: https://lists.gnu.org/r/emacs-devel/2022-09/msg00384.html

tmux now supports images, both kitty and sixel images.  If you do this,=20
you should also support sixels which are more widely supported.  What=20
tmux does when it sees an image is upload it into tmux and hold it as an=20
image and on display, output it as either sixel or kitty depending on=20
what's supported.  This way, you can have a sixel image displayed on a=20
kitty terminal and visa versa, tmux insulates you from the format.

I do not know if this will help you in adding this to emacs if emacs=20
handles all the scrolling and clipping.  emacs also puts the tty in=20
alternate screen mode so it would not take advantage of tmux's copy=20
buffer, so emacs would have to handle all of the scrolling and such.  It=20
should be possible.

There are 2 types of kitty images, I think you are thinking of the=20
unicode placeholder type where the image is split into cells (character=20
shaped blocks) which the local terminal knows which bit of the image to=20
put in that space.  What I don't know is what would happen with the=20
other type of kitty image which is called the kitty graphics protocol.

I think what you probably need to do is let people open a file=20
containing an image, either a sixel or jpg or whatever, then, uses=20
whatever image display capability is available in the terminal to=20
display them, handling clipping and scrolling.
