Eli Zaretskii <eliz@gnu.org> writes:

> Please at least wait until there's some sign of something like that
> happening in Emacs.  It is at least unfair to threaten a fork (which
> 110% sure is a blow to the community -- I'm old enough to remember how
> things were during the XEmacs schism) just because the Linux kernel
> did something we don't like or don't want to happen in our community.
>
> Bottom line, I think these concerns are very premature, and in most
> aspects simply not relevant to Emacs development.  To me, the actual
> danger is the threat to fork as a kind of "precaution" against
> problems that either didn't yet happen and are extremely unlikely to
> happen, or are happening already due to a variety of real-life
> reasons without causing any fuss.

i won't rehash the point more, but the word =E2=80=9Cthreat=E2=80=9D here i=
s incorrect,
though i understand it can seem that way from your perspective.

it isn't a threat, nor negotiation, nor point of debate. it is a
statement of intent; a fact, as much as any human intention can be fact.

the ground does not threaten the rain with getting wet, it simply does.

in my view, it was posted to this list, not as a threat, but as a
signpost: should the land become hostile, here is a place we can gather,
here is a place we can support each other. there's no better place to
post that than where we all already gather.

i would like you to consider another point of view, that is obvious to
me, but maybe not to you: while no one here wants a fork, it is
primarily the people who use human emacs that will suffer for it, at
least in the short and medium term. it is human emacs that will be
rejecting improvements that gnu emacs will accept, while gnu emacs will
have no issue with taking any patches to human emacs to improve gnu
emacs.

while we will all suffer collectively for a substantial fork, gnu emacs
will suffer less, with a larger community, recognition, and the ability
to accept some contributions that, regardless of quality, human emacs
will not be able to accept.

so, though the greater burden will be on the maintainers and users of
human emacs, the signatories and other participants have decided that
that is a price we must pay in order to abide our sense of morality and
justice. this is the very same decision making that birthed free
software itself; a proud tradition that is worthy of being upheld as the
world has changed around us and demanded we accommodate those changes in
our unending pursuit of human liberty, of which free software is but one
part in support.

> And I'm most alarmed by the suggestion that we should reject someone's
> code based on something that is unrelated to the code itself.  This is
> a step along a slippery slope that I very much hope we will never
> take.  Because the next thing we will be requested is to reject code
> of people with particular views or opinions, or people living under
> certain regimes, etc. etc.  We never did anything like that, and
> IMNSHO we shouldn't, ever.  The world out there is full of grave
> problems that need to be solved, but Emacs development is not the
> right place to solve almost all of these problems.

gnu emacs already rejects code for a number of reasons outside the code
itself. copyright assignment has already been mentioned, but you can
also see recent discussions about things in elpa potentially promoting
non-free software services. in the past, code has been rejected because
it provided features for non-free platforms that weren't available on
free ones. and so on. so gnu emacs rejects software for ideological
reasons rather than technical. i think that's a fine way to
be. technical details should always be in support of the social goals,
and, indeed, can't be separated anyway.

slippery slopes are dangerous, but the solution here, as in most things,
is constant vigilance, to make sure our beliefs are being upheld in the
constant work of creation and maintenance.

-bjc

>> > Here, for better or worse, the perspective must be a bit different.  I
>> > didn't ask an LLM to help me write some code; I just made it translate
>> > from C++ to Elisp, and keep iterating until the Hunspell tests pass and
>> > the obvious performance bottlenecks are fixed.  The code in a way
>> > doesn't matter, and I only looked cursorily at it.
>> >
>> > That's also why I intentionally replaced the docstrings with comments,
>> > so no user gets their eyes hurt with slop.
>> 
>> So you would claim that vibe-spell.el is effectively not source code,
>> but just the output of a non-conventional compilation process from C++
>> to Elisp?
>
> If this is the case, then IMO the package should include both the
> original Hunspell code used for conversion and the prompts used to
> generate the final ELisp version.  IOW, the "source code" here is some
> specific version of Hunspell, which thjus should be in the package,
> and the package should also include all the scripts and tools and
> instructions needed to regenerate the ELisp version.  Imagine the
> scenario where someone wants to cherry-pick some change in upstream
> Hunspell to this package: they will need to patch the Hunspell sources
> and then run the LLM to produce the corresponding ELisp changes.  They
> will need everything that this process requires.
>
> I believe this is a basic requirement of GPL, isn't it?

If this argument is true, it is also a clear explanation of why
vibecoded software should not be included in Emacs, since it would
require complete and corresponding code to whatever the model was
trained on, which given would bloat Emacs to the size of nearly all code
publicly released ever written, which is infeasible.

> Eli Zaretskii <eliz@gnu.org> writes:
> 
> >> So you would claim that vibe-spell.el is effectively not source code,
> >> but just the output of a non-conventional compilation process from C++
> >> to Elisp?
> >
> > If this is the case, then IMO the package should include both the
> > original Hunspell code used for conversion and the prompts used to
> > generate the final ELisp version.  IOW, the "source code" here is some
> > specific version of Hunspell, which thjus should be in the package,
> > and the package should also include all the scripts and tools and
> > instructions needed to regenerate the ELisp version.  Imagine the
> > scenario where someone wants to cherry-pick some change in upstream
> > Hunspell to this package: they will need to patch the Hunspell sources
> > and then run the LLM to produce the corresponding ELisp changes.  They
> > will need everything that this process requires.
> >
> > I believe this is a basic requirement of GPL, isn't it?
> 
> If this argument is true, it is also a clear explanation of why
> vibecoded software should not be included in Emacs, since it would
> require complete and corresponding code to whatever the model was
> trained on, which given would bloat Emacs to the size of nearly all code
> publicly released ever written, which is infeasible.

I'm not sure you are right.  It remains to be seen what is the minimum
material that needs to be part of the source code in these cases.  But
yes, it's a complicated issue, and one of the reasons why the
discussions about the legal implications of LLM-generated code take a
long time.

That's also true for C/C++ code generated by Bison, and for Texinfo
generated from an Org document (as well as in many other similar
cases), but we still require to include the original source.  I don't
understand how this case is different.

> 
> Points like these should be directed to emacs-tangents, as they will
> just spiral off into non-emacs-devel related discussions if presented
> here.

Discussions about what GPL requirements mean for software we accept
belong squarely to this list, IMO.  They directly affect Emacs
development.  They are not tangents.

Eli Zaretskii <eliz@gnu.org> writes:

>> From: Philip Kaludercic <philipk@posteo.net>
>> Cc: Eli Zaretskii <eliz@gnu.org>,  arstoffel@gmail.com,  emacs-devel@gnu.org
>> Date: Thu, 05 Nov 2026 18:19:19 +0000
>> 
>> Christine Lemmer-Webber <cwebber@dustycloud.org> writes:
>> 
>> > Eli Zaretskii <eliz@gnu.org> writes:
>> >
>> >> If this is the case, then IMO the package should include both the
>> >> original Hunspell code used for conversion and the prompts used to
>> >> generate the final ELisp version.  IOW, the "source code" here is some
>> >> specific version of Hunspell, which thjus should be in the package,
>> >> and the package should also include all the scripts and tools and
>> >> instructions needed to regenerate the ELisp version.  Imagine the
>> >> scenario where someone wants to cherry-pick some change in upstream
>> >> Hunspell to this package: they will need to patch the Hunspell sources
>> >> and then run the LLM to produce the corresponding ELisp changes.  They
>> >> will need everything that this process requires.
>> >>
>> >> I believe this is a basic requirement of GPL, isn't it?
>> >
>> > If this argument is true, 
>> 
>> FWIW the author disagrees with the framing, see
>> <87qzgzlohb.fsf@gmail.com>.  And ultimately, the source code of
>> vibe-spell.el, while unusual, is still editable as such without the user
>> needing access to the C++ source.
>
> That's also true for C/C++ code generated by Bison, and for Texinfo
> generated from an Org document (as well as in many other similar
> cases), but we still require to include the original source.  I don't
> understand how this case is different.

Assuming Augusto agrees, then vibe-spell is a fork+translation as
opposed to just a transcompilation of an existing program.  The source
code in vibe-spell.el is authoritative on its own and there is no
expectation that C++ changes can be (automatically) copied over, as the
translation is not a process that one would expect to repeat over and
over (which is the case with C/C++->Bison or Org->Texinfo, considering
that you know that the tool will overwrite your changes in the
intermediate representations).

>> >                           it is also a clear explanation of why
>> > vibecoded software should not be included in Emacs, since it would
>> > require complete and corresponding code to whatever the model was
>> > trained on, which given would bloat Emacs to the size of nearly all code
>> > publicly released ever written, which is infeasible.
>> 
>> Points like these should be directed to emacs-tangents, as they will
>> just spiral off into non-emacs-devel related discussions if presented
>> here.
>
> Discussions about what GPL requirements mean for software we accept
> belong squarely to this list, IMO.  They directly affect Emacs
> development.  They are not tangents.

Discussions on how LLM relate to GPL seem more general than just Emacs,
which was my understanding of the point that Christine was making.  But
if I make a wrong call, I apologize.

> From: bjc@spork.org
> Cc: Jim Porter <jporterbugs@gmail.com>,  bugs@gnu.support,  phil@hagelb.org,
>   emacs-devel@gnu.org
> Date: Thu, 05 Nov 2026 12:44:34 -0500
> 
> i won't rehash the point more, but the word “threat” here is incorrect,
> though i understand it can seem that way from your perspective.
> 
> it isn't a threat, nor negotiation, nor point of debate. it is a
> statement of intent; a fact, as much as any human intention can be fact.
> 
> the ground does not threaten the rain with getting wet, it simply does.

The rain does or does not wet regardless of what we say or do or
decide.  So, unless you've already decided to fork regardless of what
will be the decisions in these matters, your analogy is invalid.

> in my view, it was posted to this list, not as a threat, but as a
> signpost: should the land become hostile, here is a place we can gather,
> here is a place we can support each other. there's no better place to
> post that than where we all already gather.

No one explained what kind of hostility will happen, and people here,
myself included, don't really understand what is alluded to.  It
almost seems like you've decided that LLMs have adverse effects in
some of their popular uses, and therefore using LLM-generated code
will somehow "infect" Emacs even though those problematic aspects of
using LLMs are completely irrelevant for our case.  I personally hope
that this is not the reason, but in the absence of any other
argumentation, what else is left?

> so, though the greater burden will be on the maintainers and users of
> human emacs, the signatories and other participants have decided that
> that is a price we must pay in order to abide our sense of morality and
> justice. this is the very same decision making that birthed free
> software itself; a proud tradition that is worthy of being upheld as the
> world has changed around us and demanded we accommodate those changes in
> our unending pursuit of human liberty, of which free software is but one
> part in support.

If the principles and traditions are justified and noble, sure.
Otherwise...

> > And I'm most alarmed by the suggestion that we should reject someone's
> > code based on something that is unrelated to the code itself.  This is
> > a step along a slippery slope that I very much hope we will never
> > take.  Because the next thing we will be requested is to reject code
> > of people with particular views or opinions, or people living under
> > certain regimes, etc. etc.  We never did anything like that, and
> > IMNSHO we shouldn't, ever.  The world out there is full of grave
> > problems that need to be solved, but Emacs development is not the
> > right place to solve almost all of these problems.
> 
> gnu emacs already rejects code for a number of reasons outside the code
> itself. copyright assignment has already been mentioned, but you can
> also see recent discussions about things in elpa potentially promoting
> non-free software services.

That's very different.  Here, we are asked to reject a completely
valid, free and correct code just because of its progeny.  It's like
saying that code from left-handed or red-haired people should not be
accepted.
